```c/c++
// Copyright Artur. AZ project.

#include "Animation/AZ_MoverAnimInstance.h"

#include "Animation/AnimSequenceBase.h"
#include "BlendStack/BlendStackAnimNodeLibrary.h"
#include "Character/AZ_PawnMoverComponent.h"
#include "Character/AZ_PawnMoverHeroCharacter.h"
#include "DefaultMovementSet/CharacterMoverComponent.h"
#include "DefaultMovementSet/LayeredMoves/RootMotionAttributeLayeredMove.h"
#include "MoverDataModelTypes.h"   // FCharacterDefaultInputs
#include "Engine/Engine.h"
#include "Engine/World.h"
#include "MoverComponent.h"
#include "MoverPoseSearchTrajectoryPredictor.h"
#include "PoseSearch/PoseSearchLibrary.h"
#include "PoseSearch/PoseSearchTrajectoryLibrary.h"
#include "PoseSearch/PoseSearchTrajectoryPredictor.h"

void UAZ_MoverAnimInstance::NativeInitializeAnimation()
{
	Super::NativeInitializeAnimation();

	// RM bridge (anim side). Mover does NOT consume UAnimInstance::RootMotionMode directly, but
	// RootMotionFromEverything makes the engine extract the per-frame root delta from ALL playing
	// anims into the "RootMotionDelta" mesh attribute. FLayeredMove_RootMotionAttribute (queued on
	// the pawn's MoverComp in BeginPlay) consumes that attribute to move the capsule. Loops carry
	// ~zero authored RM, so this only drives stop/start/transition clips. Mirrors v1 UAZ_AnimInstance.
	// See project_root_motion_mode.
	RootMotionMode = ERootMotionMode::RootMotionFromEverything;

	// Cache pawn + Mover refs — pawn class is fixed at spawn, no need to re-cast every tick.
	if (APawn* PawnOwner = TryGetPawnOwner())
	{
		CachedPawn = Cast<AAZ_PawnMoverHeroCharacter>(PawnOwner);
		if (CachedPawn)
		{
			CachedMover = CachedPawn->GetMoverComponent();
			CachedCMC = Cast<UCharacterMoverComponent>(CachedMover);
		}
	}
}

void UAZ_MoverAnimInstance::NativeUpdateAnimation(float DeltaSeconds)
{
	Super::NativeUpdateAnimation(DeltaSeconds);

	if (!CachedPawn || !CachedMover)
	{
		return;
	}

	// ---- A′ per-transition RM bridge (game thread) ----
	// SetBlendStackAnimFromChooser (possibly on the anim worker thread) flags this when it pushes a
	// start/stop clip. Queue the OverrideAll root-motion-attribute move here, on the game thread, with
	// DurationMs = the transition clip's remaining length so it auto-expires when the transition ends.
	// Loops queue nothing → the RootMotionDelta attribute is written but unconsumed → velocity-driven.
	// See project_root_motion_mode (approach A′).
	if (bPendingTransitionRMMove)
	{
		bPendingTransitionRMMove = false;
		TSharedPtr<FLayeredMove_RootMotionAttribute> RMMove = MakeShared<FLayeredMove_RootMotionAttribute>();
		RMMove->DurationMs = PendingTransitionRMMoveDurationMs;
		CachedMover->QueueLayeredMove(RMMove);
	}

	const EAZ_StateMachineState PreviousSMState = ChooserContext.SMState;

	// ---- Trajectory (option A — PoseSearch FTransformTrajectory via the Mover predictor) ----
	// SINGLE source: feeds the AnimGraph PoseHistory node (MM) via the "Trajectory" property binding AND
	// the intent-based IsMoving below. Mirrors v1 Update_Trajectory. The predictor is Setup on the pawn in
	// BeginPlay. Future velocity is finite-differenced from the predicted samples (the sample struct carries
	// position/facing/time, not velocity), giving a leading indicator that catches start/stop on intent.
	if (UMoverTrajectoryPredictor* Predictor = CachedPawn->GetTrajectoryPredictor())
	{
		TScriptInterface<IPoseSearchTrajectoryPredictorInterface> PredictorInterface;
		PredictorInterface.SetObject(Predictor);
		PredictorInterface.SetInterface(Cast<IPoseSearchTrajectoryPredictorInterface>(Predictor));

		UPoseSearchTrajectoryLibrary::PoseSearchGenerateTransformTrajectoryWithPredictor(
			PredictorInterface, DeltaSeconds, Trajectory, PredictionYawLast, Trajectory,
			/*HistoryInterval*/ 0.04f, /*HistoryCount*/ 10,
			/*PredictionInterval*/ 0.1f, /*PredictionCount*/ 10);

		const FVector PNow = Trajectory.GetSampleAtTime(0.f).Position;
		const FVector PFut = Trajectory.GetSampleAtTime(TrajectoryFutureLookahead).Position;
		PredictedFutureVelocity = (PFut - PNow) / FMath::Max(0.01f, TrajectoryFutureLookahead);
	}

	// ---- Mover-derived fields ----
	const FVector Velocity = CachedMover->GetVelocity();
	ChooserContext.Speed2D = static_cast<float>(Velocity.Size2D());   // measured speed (gait thresholds, leaning)

	// Intent-based "is moving" — keyed on the player's ACTUAL movement INPUT, not predicted/measured
	// velocity. During an RM-driven stop (and the velocity-driven decel of the walk loop) the capsule is
	// still moving forward, so a velocity-based test keeps IsMoving true → the SM never leaves
	// LocomotionLoop / oscillates (the WalkFwdLoop "stuck loop" bug). Reading the last Mover input cmd makes
	// release→stop fire on intent (the frame W is released), with the RM stop clip then driving the decel.
	// (GASP: IsMoving = future velocity + accel; input intent is the cleanest accel/intent proxy and works
	// for start AND stop.) PredictedFutureVelocity is still used below for MovementDirection + MM trajectory.
	FVector MoveIntentWS = FVector::ZeroVector;
	{
		const FMoverInputCmdContext& LastInput = CachedMover->GetLastInputCmd();
		if (const FCharacterDefaultInputs* CharIn = LastInput.InputCollection.FindDataByType<FCharacterDefaultInputs>())
		{
			MoveIntentWS = CharIn->GetMoveInput_WorldSpace();
		}
	}
	ChooserContext.bIsMoving = MoveIntentWS.SizeSquared2D() > FMath::Square(0.1f);   // small input deadzone

	// MovementMode from the active mode's registered name (set on the MoverComponent's MovementModes map).
	const FName ModeName = CachedMover->GetMovementModeName();
	if (ModeName == TEXT("Walking"))
	{
		ChooserContext.MovementMode = EAZ_MovementMode::OnGround;
	}
	else if (ModeName == TEXT("Falling"))
	{
		ChooserContext.MovementMode = EAZ_MovementMode::InAir;
	}
	// Slide / Swim left as default until those modes exist.

	// Stance — CharacterMoverComponent owns crouch state.
	ChooserContext.Stance = (CachedCMC && CachedCMC->IsCrouching())
		? EAZ_Stance::Crouching
		: EAZ_Stance::Standing;

	// Gait — derived from speed for now. When a sprint input + gait threshold layer lands
	// (Step 5 follow-up), replace this with a read from the pawn's cached input intent.
	const FVector CurrentForward = CachedPawn->GetActorForwardVector();
	if (ChooserContext.Speed2D > 500.f)        ChooserContext.Gait = EAZ_Gait::Sprint;
	else if (ChooserContext.Speed2D > 200.f)   ChooserContext.Gait = EAZ_Gait::Run;
	else                                       ChooserContext.Gait = EAZ_Gait::Walk;

	// MovementDirection — relative to actor forward. Cheap dot/sign decision for F/B/L/R.
	if (ChooserContext.bIsMoving)
	{
		const FVector VelDir2D = PredictedFutureVelocity.GetSafeNormal2D();   // intent direction (future, not lagging current)
		const float Forward = static_cast<float>(FVector::DotProduct(VelDir2D, CurrentForward));
		const float Right   = static_cast<float>(FVector::DotProduct(VelDir2D, CachedPawn->GetActorRightVector()));
		if (FMath::Abs(Forward) > FMath::Abs(Right))
		{
			ChooserContext.MovementDirection = Forward >= 0.f ? EAZ_MovementDirection::F : EAZ_MovementDirection::B;
		}
		else
		{
			// L/R reported without foot-lead specificity (LL / RR) until we wire the
			// foot-leading classifier — the GASP-parity 4-way split (LL/LR/RL/RR) needs
			// a foot-phase signal we don't have yet. Chooser rows for first-pass
			// locomotion can collapse LL+LR / RL+RR with the MultiEnum column.
			ChooserContext.MovementDirection = Right >= 0.f ? EAZ_MovementDirection::RR : EAZ_MovementDirection::LL;
		}
	}
	// When idle, leave MovementDirection as last computed — chooser rows for IdleLoop use Any.

	// Planted-foot — read the contact_l curve from the playing clip (baked 0/1 step, threshold 0.5).
	// Same convention as v1 UAZ_AnimInstance. Drives the BoolColumn that selects L/R stop/start
	// variants; false when the current clip carries no contact curve (idle, break, jump).
	ChooserContext.bLeftFootDown = GetCurveValue(FName(TEXT("contact_l"))) > 0.5f;

	// ---- GAS tag snapshot from the pawn (routes through IGameplayTagAssetInterface → ASC). ----
	ChooserContext.OwnedTags.Reset();
	CachedPawn->GetOwnedGameplayTags(ChooserContext.OwnedTags);

	// ---- Camera/facing — for rotation-aware chooser rows (TIP, AO chains) ----
	if (const AController* Controller = CachedPawn->GetController())
	{
		ChooserContext.AimingRotation = Controller->GetControlRotation();
	}
	// RotationOffset is signed delta from actor yaw to camera yaw (radians or degrees?
	// Project convention: degrees. Matches FAZ_MoverCustomInputs::RotationOffset units.)
	ChooserContext.RotationOffset = FRotator::NormalizeAxis(
		ChooserContext.AimingRotation.Yaw - CachedPawn->GetActorRotation().Yaw);

#if !UE_BUILD_SHIPPING
	if (bDebugTrajectory && GEngine)
	{
		GEngine->AddOnScreenDebugMessage(reinterpret_cast<uint64>(this), 0.f, FColor::Cyan,
			FString::Printf(TEXT("[v2 Trajectory] FutureVel2D=%.0f  (curVel2D=%.0f)  samples=%d"),
				PredictedFutureVelocity.Size2D(), ChooserContext.Speed2D, Trajectory.Samples.Num()));
		// Foot diagnostic: are the walk loop's contact curves reaching GetCurveValue at runtime?
		// If cL/cR stay 0.00 while walking, the BlendStack isn't propagating curves (→ bLeftFootDown
		// always False → always _LU). If they swing 0↔1, the signal works and we look elsewhere.
		GEngine->AddOnScreenDebugMessage(reinterpret_cast<uint64>(this) + 1, 0.f, FColor::Yellow,
			FString::Printf(TEXT("[v2 Foot] contact_l=%.2f contact_r=%.2f  bLeftFootDown=%d  SM=%d  bIsMoving=%d"),
				GetCurveValue(FName(TEXT("contact_l"))), GetCurveValue(FName(TEXT("contact_r"))),
				ChooserContext.bLeftFootDown ? 1 : 0,
				static_cast<int32>(ChooserContext.SMState), ChooserContext.bIsMoving ? 1 : 0));
	}
#endif

	// ---- Phase derivation (the "C++ SM") — see project_v2_architecture.md ----
	ChooserContext.SMState = DeriveSMState(ChooserContext, PreviousSMState);

	// A′ RM-move teardown: whenever we leave a transition phase — whether it completed normally or was
	// abandoned early (e.g. input re-pressed mid-stop) — cancel the per-transition RM move so it can't keep
	// OverrideAll-driving the resumed loop until its DurationMs auto-expires. Our move is tagged
	// Mover.AnimRootMotion.MeshAttribute (child of Mover_AnimRootMotion); a non-exact cancel matches it.
	// No-op when no such move is active. (CancelFeaturesWithTag only touches layered moves/modifiers.)
	auto IsTransitionPhase = [](EAZ_StateMachineState S)
	{
		return S == EAZ_StateMachineState::TransitionToIdle || S == EAZ_StateMachineState::TransitionToLocomotion;
	};
	if (IsTransitionPhase(PreviousSMState) && !IsTransitionPhase(ChooserContext.SMState))
	{
		CachedMover->CancelFeaturesWithTag(Mover_AnimRootMotion, /*bRequireExactMatch*/ false);
	}
}

void UAZ_MoverAnimInstance::SetBlendStackAnimFromChooser(
	EAZ_StateMachineState State,
	bool bForceBlend,
	FAnimNodeReference BlendStackNode,
	FAZ_ChooserOutputs ChooserOut,
	UAnimationAsset* ChosenAnim,
	const TArray<UObject*>& Candidates)
{
	// NOTE: do NOT assign ChooserContext.SMState from `State`. DeriveSMState (called from
	// NativeUpdateAnimation) is the single source of truth for the SM phase. The `State`
	// parameter is kept for BP-signature stability but is advisory only — assigning it back
	// here would let an EventGraph wiring (e.g. literal IdleLoop on the pin) clobber the
	// derived phase, causing IdleBreak to flash for one tick then revert to IdleLoop.
	ChooserOutputs = ChooserOut;

	// Nothing to play: first-match mode gives ChosenAnim, return-all mode gives Candidates.
	// Bail only when both are empty.
	if (!ChosenAnim && Candidates.Num() == 0)
	{
		return;
	}

	// Transition clip lock: once a start/stop clip is pushed, let it play to completion (DeriveSMState's
	// TransitionEndTime governs the fall-through to the target loop). Without this, the stop clip's own
	// contact_l curve would keep flipping bLeftFootDown, re-trigger the chooser, and restart the stop on
	// the other foot every few frames. The entry frame (SMState just changed from the loop) is NOT locked
	// — only subsequent frames where we're still in the same transition.
	const bool bInTransition =
		ChooserContext.SMState == EAZ_StateMachineState::TransitionToIdle ||
		ChooserContext.SMState == EAZ_StateMachineState::TransitionToLocomotion;
	if (bInTransition && ChooserContext.SMState == LastPushedSMState && !bForceBlend)
	{
		return;
	}

	// Short-circuit: if the chooser's selection inputs haven't changed since the last
	// successful push, skip re-pushing to BlendStack. Without this, RandomizeColumn rows
	// (e.g. 3 idle-break variants) churn the stack every tick because the random pick
	// re-rolls per evaluation. Bypassed when:
	//   - bForceBlend is true (caller explicitly asked for a fresh blend)
	//   - bUseMM is true (motion matching intentionally re-picks each frame)
	const bool bSelectionChanged =
		ChooserContext.SMState           != LastPushedSMState ||
		ChooserContext.Stance            != LastPushedStance ||
		ChooserContext.Gait              != LastPushedGait ||
		ChooserContext.MovementDirection != LastPushedDir ||
		ChooserContext.bLeftFootDown     != LastPushedLeftFootDown;

	if (!bSelectionChanged && !bForceBlend && !ChooserOut.bUseMM && BlendStackInputs.Anim != nullptr)
	{
		return;
	}

	LastPushedSMState        = ChooserContext.SMState;
	LastPushedStance         = ChooserContext.Stance;
	LastPushedGait           = ChooserContext.Gait;
	LastPushedDir            = ChooserContext.MovementDirection;
	LastPushedLeftFootDown   = ChooserContext.bLeftFootDown;

	bool bAssetLooping = false;

	if (ChooserOut.bUseMM)
	{
		// MotionMatch over the chooser's candidate set — picks both the clip (when several are
		// supplied) and the best entry frame by current pose+trajectory cost. When the chooser
		// runs in first-match mode it hands us one anim via ChosenAnim and an empty Candidates
		// array, so fall back to searching just {ChosenAnim}. Requires a PoseHistory node tagged
		// "PoseHistory" in the AnimGraph.
		TArray<UObject*> AssetsToSearch = Candidates;
		if (AssetsToSearch.Num() == 0)
		{
			AssetsToSearch.Add(ChosenAnim);
		}

		FPoseSearchBlueprintResult MMResult;
		UPoseSearchLibrary::MotionMatch(
			this, AssetsToSearch, FName("PoseHistory"),
			FPoseSearchContinuingProperties(), FPoseSearchFutureProperties(),
			MMResult);

		UAnimationAsset* MMAnim = Cast<UAnimationAsset>(MMResult.SelectedAnim);
		if (!MMAnim)
		{
			return;
		}

		// MMCostLimit: when > 0, MM result must beat that cost or we keep current anim.
		if (ChooserOut.MMCostLimit > 0.0 && MMResult.SearchCost > ChooserOut.MMCostLimit)
		{
			return;
		}

		UPoseSearchLibrary::IsAnimationAssetLooping(MMAnim, bAssetLooping);

		BlendStackInputs.Anim         = MMAnim;
		BlendStackInputs.bLoop        = bAssetLooping;
		BlendStackInputs.StartTime    = MMResult.SelectedTime;
		BlendStackInputs.BlendTime    = ChooserOut.BlendTime;
		BlendStackInputs.BlendProfile = const_cast<UBlendProfile*>(GetBlendProfileByName(ChooserOut.BlendProfile));
		BlendStackInputs.Tags         = ChooserOut.Tags;
	}
	else
	{
		// Non-MM path: take the chooser-supplied asset + timing/profile/tags directly.
		// Prefer the single Result pin; fall back to the first candidate if only the array is wired.
		UAnimationAsset* DirectAnim = ChosenAnim ? ChosenAnim : Cast<UAnimationAsset>(Candidates[0]);
		if (!DirectAnim)
		{
			return;
		}
		UPoseSearchLibrary::IsAnimationAssetLooping(DirectAnim, bAssetLooping);

		BlendStackInputs.Anim         = DirectAnim;
		BlendStackInputs.bLoop        = bAssetLooping;
		BlendStackInputs.StartTime    = ChooserOut.StartTime;
		BlendStackInputs.BlendTime    = ChooserOut.BlendTime;
		BlendStackInputs.BlendProfile = const_cast<UBlendProfile*>(GetBlendProfileByName(ChooserOut.BlendProfile));
		BlendStackInputs.Tags         = ChooserOut.Tags;
	}

	// Idle-break duration tracking — when an IdleBreak anim is pushed, record when DeriveSMState
	// should flip back to IdleLoop. Using world time + (anim length - threshold) lets the
	// BlendStack cross-fade begin before the break anim's last frame, hiding the cut.
	// Reads ChooserContext.SMState (not the parameter) so a misconfigured EventGraph pin
	// can't suppress break tracking.
	if (ChooserContext.SMState == EAZ_StateMachineState::IdleBreak)
	{
		if (const UAnimSequenceBase* Seq = Cast<UAnimSequenceBase>(BlendStackInputs.Anim))
		{
			const UWorld* World = GetWorld();
			const float Now = World ? World->GetTimeSeconds() : 0.f;
			IdleBreakEndTime = Now + Seq->GetPlayLength() - IdleBreakAlmostCompleteThreshold;
		}
	}

	// Transition (start/stop) tracking + A′ RM-bridge handoff. Reached only on the transition's ENTRY
	// frame (subsequent frames hit the transition lock above and return early). (1) Schedule the
	// fall-through to the target loop, and (2) flag NativeUpdateAnimation (game thread) to queue the
	// per-transition RM move for the clip's REMAINING length (Len - StartTime — StartTime is non-zero
	// when MM picks an entry frame).
	if (bInTransition)
	{
#if !UE_BUILD_SHIPPING
		// DIAGNOSTIC: what foot did the chooser see, and what clip did it pick? (always-_LU debug)
		UE_LOG(LogTemp, Warning, TEXT("[v2 STOP] entry: ctx.bLeftFootDown=%d Dir=%d Gait=%d -> chosen=%s"),
			ChooserContext.bLeftFootDown ? 1 : 0, static_cast<int32>(ChooserContext.MovementDirection),
			static_cast<int32>(ChooserContext.Gait), *GetNameSafe(BlendStackInputs.Anim));
#endif
		if (const UAnimSequenceBase* Seq = Cast<UAnimSequenceBase>(BlendStackInputs.Anim))
		{
			const UWorld* World = GetWorld();
			const float Now = World ? World->GetTimeSeconds() : 0.f;
			const float Remaining = FMath::Max(0.05f,
				Seq->GetPlayLength() - static_cast<float>(BlendStackInputs.StartTime));
			TransitionEndTime = Now + Remaining - TransitionAlmostCompleteThreshold;
			PendingTransitionRMMoveDurationMs = Remaining * 1000.f;
			bPendingTransitionRMMove = true;
		}
	}

	// Force a fresh blend if the caller requested it AND we're not playing the same loop
	// (re-entering the same loop should not re-blend — matches v1's pivot-failure carve-out).
	if (bForceBlend && !BlendStackInputs.bLoop)
	{
		EAnimNodeReferenceConversionResult ConvResult;
		const FBlendStackAnimNodeReference BSNode =
			UBlendStackAnimNodeLibrary::ConvertToBlendStackNode(BlendStackNode, ConvResult);
		if (ConvResult == EAnimNodeReferenceConversionResult::Succeeded)
		{
			UBlendStackAnimNodeLibrary::ForceBlendNextUpdate(BSNode);
		}
	}
}

EAZ_StateMachineState UAZ_MoverAnimInstance::DeriveSMState(
	const FAZ_v2_ChooserContext& Current, EAZ_StateMachineState Previous)
{
	// Locomotion phase, NOT posture/intent. Tags handle the orthogonal "aiming/reloading/etc"
	// dimension via OwnedTags; SMState is purely "where am I in the move cycle?".
	//
	// Transition states (TransitionToIdle, TransitionToLocomotion) deferred until we have
	// the per-anim chooser rows + motion-matching DBs that drive them.

	// Non-idle phases — clear any pending idle-break / transition scheduling.
	if (Current.MovementMode == EAZ_MovementMode::InAir)
	{
		NextIdleBreakTime = -1.f;
		IdleBreakEndTime  = -1.f;
		TransitionEndTime = -1.f;
		return EAZ_StateMachineState::InAirLoop;
	}
	if (Current.bIsMoving)
	{
		// Moving (intent). Re-pressing input mid-stop lands here → resume the loop immediately,
		// abandoning the stop transition. (Start transition deferred to a later iteration.)
		NextIdleBreakTime = -1.f;
		IdleBreakEndTime  = -1.f;
		TransitionEndTime = -1.f;
		return EAZ_StateMachineState::LocomotionLoop;
	}

	const UWorld* World = GetWorld();
	const float Now = World ? World->GetTimeSeconds() : 0.f;

	// --- Stop transition: moving → idle routes through TransitionToIdle (plays a foot-aware RM stop
	// clip) before settling to IdleLoop. TransitionEndTime is populated by SetBlendStackAnimFromChooser
	// from the chosen clip's length (same mechanism as IdleBreakEndTime). ---
	if (Previous == EAZ_StateMachineState::LocomotionLoop)
	{
		// Arm a default fall-through, overridden by the real clip length in SetBlendStackAnimFromChooser.
		// Safety net: if no TransitionToIdle chooser row matches (so no clip is pushed), this still lets
		// us settle to idle instead of freezing in the stop phase.
		TransitionEndTime = Now + 1.0f;
		return EAZ_StateMachineState::TransitionToIdle;
	}
	if (Previous == EAZ_StateMachineState::TransitionToIdle)
	{
		if (TransitionEndTime > 0.f && Now >= TransitionEndTime)
		{
			TransitionEndTime = -1.f;
			NextIdleBreakTime = Now + FMath::FRandRange(IdleBreakMinTime, IdleBreakMaxTime);
			return EAZ_StateMachineState::IdleLoop;
		}
		return EAZ_StateMachineState::TransitionToIdle;
	}

	// Already mid-break — stay until the break anim's almost-complete window. IdleBreakEndTime
	// is populated by SetBlendStackAnimFromChooser when the chosen break anim is pushed.
	if (Previous == EAZ_StateMachineState::IdleBreak)
	{
		if (IdleBreakEndTime > 0.f && Now >= IdleBreakEndTime)
		{
			IdleBreakEndTime = -1.f;
			NextIdleBreakTime = Now + FMath::FRandRange(IdleBreakMinTime, IdleBreakMaxTime);
			return EAZ_StateMachineState::IdleLoop;
		}
		return EAZ_StateMachineState::IdleBreak;
	}

	// IdleLoop or fresh entry into idle. Schedule the next break if none pending, otherwise
	// fire when the scheduled time is reached.
	if (NextIdleBreakTime < 0.f)
	{
		NextIdleBreakTime = Now + FMath::FRandRange(IdleBreakMinTime, IdleBreakMaxTime);
	}
	else if (Now >= NextIdleBreakTime)
	{
		NextIdleBreakTime = -1.f;
		return EAZ_StateMachineState::IdleBreak;
	}
	return EAZ_StateMachineState::IdleLoop;
}

```

# Iris Replication vs. Actor Replication in UE5

These are two different network replication systems in Unreal Engine 5. Here's a breakdown of the key differences:

## Actor Replication (The Legacy/Classic System)

This is the original replication system that has been in Unreal Engine since the early days.

### How it works
- Built around the **`UNetDriver`** + **`UActorChannel`** + **`FObjectReplicator`** model.
- Each replicated actor gets an **actor channel** per connection.
- Properties marked with `UPROPERTY(Replicated)` are compared against a per-connection shadow state during `ReplicateActor()`.
- Uses **`GetLifetimeReplicatedProps()`** + `DOREPLIFETIME` macros to declare what replicates.
- RPCs and property replication flow through the same channel/bunch mechanism.

### Characteristics
- **Per-connection** property comparison (the server compares state for *every* connection separately → O(connections × properties)).
- "Push" only happens during the actor's net update; relies on dirty-checking via comparison.
- Mature, battle-tested, supported everywhere.

---

## Iris Replication (The New System)

Iris is a **newer, modern replication system** introduced as experimental in UE5.1 and matured through later versions. It's designed to scale to large numbers of actors/players (think large-scale multiplayer).

### How it works
- Replaces the channel-per-actor model with a centralized **`UReplicationSystem`**.
- Uses **`NetSerializers`** and **descriptors** generated from the property metadata to serialize state.
- Relies heavily on **push-based dirtiness** — code explicitly marks state dirty (`MarkNetDirty` / push model) rather than the system polling/comparing every property.
- State is stored in a more cache-friendly, data-oriented layout.

### Characteristics
- **Push-model first**: dirtiness is tracked once, not re-derived per connection — much better CPU scaling with many connections.
- **Data-oriented design** for better cache coherency and parallelism.
- Centralized filtering, prioritization, and bandwidth management.
- Designed for **scalability** (large player counts / many replicated objects).

---

## Key Differences at a Glance

| Aspect | Actor Replication (Classic) | Iris |
|---|---|---|
| Architecture | Per-actor channels (`UActorChannel`) | Centralized `UReplicationSystem` |
| Dirty detection | Comparison/polling (per connection) | Push-based (mark dirty once) |
| Data layout | Object-oriented, scattered | Data-oriented, cache-friendly |
| CPU scaling | Scales poorly with many connections | Scales much better |
| Maturity | Fully mature, default | Newer (was experimental, maturing) |
| API to declare | `GetLifetimeReplicatedProps` / `DOREPLIFETIME` | Same `Replicated` macros + NetSerializers (largely compatible) |

---

## Practical Notes

- **API compatibility**: Iris was designed to be largely **drop-in compatible** with existing `UPROPERTY(Replicated)` declarations — you generally don't rewrite your replicated properties. It can be enabled/disabled per project.
- **Push model**: Even on classic replication, you can opt into the push model (`EPushModelState`), but Iris embraces it as a core design principle.
- **When to use Iris**: It really shines for projects targeting **high concurrent player counts** or **large numbers of replicated objects** where the per-connection comparison cost of classic replication becomes a bottleneck.

---

Since you're on **UE 5.8.0 built from source**, you have access to a fairly mature version of Iris. If you'd like, I can:

1. Check whether **Iris is currently enabled** in your project's config (`DefaultEngine.ini` / build settings), or
2. Look at how your replicated actors are declared to advise on Iris compatibility.

Would you like me to investigate either of those in your project?

# Actor Replication: The `UNetDriver` + `UActorChannel` + `FObjectReplicator` Model

This is the **classic** (pre-Iris) replication pipeline. Let me build it up layer by layer, top-down, so you see how each piece fits.

---

## The 10,000-foot view

When the server wants to keep a client's world in sync, this happens **every server tick** (roughly):

```
UNetDriver::TickFlush()
   └─> ServerReplicateActors()              // decide WHAT to send, to WHOM
        └─> for each connection (UNetConnection)
             └─> for each relevant actor
                  └─> UActorChannel::ReplicateActor()   // the actor's "pipe" on that connection
                       └─> FObjectReplicator::ReplicateProperties()  // diff + serialize props
                            └─> writes changed properties into an FOutBunch
```

Then bunches get packed into packets and sent over the wire. Let me explain each actor in that play.

---

## 1. `UNetDriver` — the orchestrator

There is (typically) **one `UNetDriver` per world** for game traffic (the "GameNetDriver"). It owns the whole replication process.

**Responsibilities:**
- Owns the list of **`UNetConnection`** objects (one per connected client; on the client, one connection to the server).
- Drives the per-tick replication loop via `TickFlush()` → `ServerReplicateActors()`.
- Maintains the **network relevancy** and **prioritization** decisions: which actors does each connection need, and in what order (since bandwidth is limited).
- Owns the **`UReplicationGraph`** (if used) or the legacy relevancy logic.
- Manages `GuidCache` (`FNetGUIDCache`) — the mapping of network GUIDs ↔ objects so both ends agree on what object an ID refers to.

**Key idea:** `UNetDriver::ServerReplicateActors()` is where the server iterates over connections and actors and decides *what to replicate this frame*. It does dormancy checks, relevancy checks, prioritization (NetPriority), and budgeting (you can't send everything every frame).

---

## 2. `UNetConnection` — one client's link

Each connected player = one `UNetConnection` on the server. It represents the transport-level link **plus** all the per-connection replication bookkeeping.

**Responsibilities:**
- Owns the set of open **channels** (`UChannel` subclasses). Channels are multiplexed over the single connection.
- Manages the **`FOutBunch`/`FInBunch`** flow — bunches are the logical units of data; the connection packs them into UDP packets, handles ordering, reliability, ACKs, and resends.
- Holds per-connection state like the `ActorChannels` map (Actor → its `UActorChannel` on *this* connection).

This is why classic replication scales poorly with player count: **state diffing happens per connection**. If 100 players see the same actor, the work to figure out "what changed" is largely repeated 100 times.

---

## 3. `UActorChannel` — the actor's pipe on a connection

A **channel** is a logical sub-stream within a connection. The most important type is `UActorChannel`. There is **one `UActorChannel` per (Actor, Connection)** pair.

So if Actor A is relevant to 3 connections, there are 3 `UActorChannel`s for it (one inside each `UNetConnection`).

**Responsibilities:**
- Owns the **`FObjectReplicator`** for the actor *and* one for each of the actor's replicated **subobjects/components**.
- `ReplicateActor()` is the heart: it asks each `FObjectReplicator` to serialize changed properties, writes the actor's spawn info on first replication, and bundles everything into an `FOutBunch`.
- Handles **actor lifecycle** over the network: opening the channel = "spawn this actor on the client", closing it = "destroy / make irrelevant".
- Routes incoming RPCs and property updates on the receiving side.

Other channel types you'll meet: `UControlChannel` (channel 0, handshake/login), `UVoiceChannel`.

---

## 4. `FObjectReplicator` — the property diffing engine

This is where the actual **"what changed?"** work happens, for a **single `UObject`** (the actor, or one of its replicated subobjects).

**Responsibilities:**
- Holds a **shadow/“retirement” buffer** — a copy of the last-sent values of all replicated properties for this object **on this connection**.
- `ReplicateProperties()` walks the object's replicated properties (driven by the `FRepLayout`), **compares** current values to the shadow buffer, and for each property that changed, serializes the new value + its property handle into the bunch.
- Handles **RepNotify** (`OnRep_` functions): records which properties changed so the receiver can fire the notify callbacks.
- Manages **conditional replication** (the `COND_` conditions — `COND_OwnerOnly`, `COND_SkipOwner`, etc.) via the lifetime conditions.

### Supporting cast around `FObjectReplicator`

- **`FRepLayout`** — a cached, flattened description of an object class's replicated properties (offsets, types, array handling, conditions). Built once per class and shared. This is what makes the property walk fast.
- **`FRepState` / `FRepChangelistState`** — the per-object/per-connection change-tracking state (shadow data, history of changelists for reliability).
- **`FRepChangedPropertyTracker`** — tracks active/inactive property conditions and (for push model) dirty flags.

---

## How a property gets declared and flows through

You already write this part:

```cpp
// In the header
UPROPERTY(ReplicatedUsing = OnRep_Health)
float Health;

// In the .cpp
void AMyActor::GetLifetimeReplicatedProps(TArray<FLifetimeProperty>& OutLifetimeProps) const
{
    Super::GetLifetimeReplicatedProps(OutLifetimeProps);
    DOREPLIFETIME(AMyActor, Health);
}
```

What happens with that:

1. `GetLifetimeReplicatedProps` is read **once per class** to build the `FRepLayout` and the list of `FLifetimeProperty` (which holds the `COND_` condition + push model setting per property).
2. Each tick, for each connection where the actor is relevant, `FObjectReplicator::ReplicateProperties()` diffs `Health` against that connection's shadow copy.
3. If it changed → serialize the new value + handle into the `FOutBunch`.
4. On the client, the value is applied and `OnRep_Health` is fired.

---

## The "comparison" cost (why this matters for the Iris comparison later)

In the **classic** model, the default is a **poll/compare** approach:

> Every replication pass, `FObjectReplicator` *re-reads every replicated property* and *compares it* to the shadow buffer to discover changes.

This is `O(connections × replicated_properties)` of comparison work. The **Push Model** (`UEPushModelPrivate`, enabled with `EPushModelState` / `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY`) optimizes the *comparison* part by letting code say "this property changed" explicitly — but the **per-connection serialization/channel structure stays the same**. Iris later restructures the whole thing to be push-first and data-oriented. We'll get to that.

---

## Summary diagram

```
UNetDriver  (1 per world)
 ├─ FNetGUIDCache              (object ↔ NetGUID mapping)
 ├─ UReplicationGraph?         (relevancy/prioritization, optional)
 └─ UNetConnection  (1 per client)
     ├─ FOutBunch/FInBunch packing, ACKs, reliability
     └─ UActorChannel  (1 per Actor on this connection)
         ├─ FObjectReplicator  (for the Actor)
         │    ├─ FRepLayout         (shared per class: flattened prop list)
         │    ├─ Shadow buffer      (last-sent values)
         │    └─ ReplicateProperties() → diff → FOutBunch
         └─ FObjectReplicator  (for each replicated component/subobject)
```

---

This is the structural backbone. The natural next sub-topics to go deep on are:

1. **`ServerReplicateActors()` in detail** — relevancy, dormancy, `NetPriority`, and the per-frame actor budget.
2. **`FRepLayout` + `FObjectReplicator::ReplicateProperties()`** — exactly how the diff and serialization work (handles, changelists, RepNotify).
3. **Bunches & channels** — how `FOutBunch` data becomes reliable/ordered packets.
4. **RPCs** — how `Server`/`Client`/`Multicast` functions ride the same channel.
5. **Push Model** — the bridge concept toward Iris.

Which of these would you like to dig into next? I'd suggest **#2 (`FObjectReplicator` / `FRepLayout` diffing)** since that's the literal core of "what gets replicated," but I'm happy to follow your interest.

Great question — Push Model is one of the most practical optimizations in the classic replication system. Let me ground this in the actual engine source so you get the exact macros, build flags, and config for your UE 5.8 build.Let me look at a complete real example from the engine (PlayerState is a clean, canonical one) plus the build setting that enables the feature.Let me look at a real engine example to show you the exact pattern, since `APlayerState::Score` is a clean, canonical push-model property.Perfect — I found a real, canonical example right in your engine source: `APlayerState`. Let me show you exactly how it's done there, then explain the moving parts.

# Enabling Push Model

Push Model has **two parts** that must *both* be in place:

1. **Build/config**: the push model code must be compiled in **and** the CVar enabled.
2. **Code**: each property must be declared as push-based **and** you must call `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY` whenever you change it.

If you do part 2 but not part 1 (or vice versa), it silently falls back to classic poll-comparison.

---

## Step 1 — Build/config flags

### Build flag (compile-time)
Push model support is gated behind a build define. In your `*.Target.cs` (or it's on by default in editor/dev builds):

```csharp
// In YourGame.Target.cs / YourGameEditor.Target.cs
bWithPushModel = true;
```

In current UE versions this is enabled by default for editor and development builds, so usually you don't need to touch it. It's typically **compiled out of shipping** unless you opt in.

### CVar (runtime)
```ini
; DefaultEngine.ini
[SystemSettings]
net.IsPushModelEnabled=1
```
Or at runtime in the console:
```
net.IsPushModelEnabled 1
```

This is the master switch. With it `0`, even push-marked properties fall back to comparison.

---

## Step 2 — Declare properties as push-based

This is the part that lives in your `GetLifetimeReplicatedProps`. Here's the exact pattern from `APlayerState` in your engine source (`PlayerState.cpp`):

```cpp
void APlayerState::GetLifetimeReplicatedProps(TArray<FLifetimeProperty>& OutLifetimeProps) const
{
    Super::GetLifetimeReplicatedProps(OutLifetimeProps);

    FDoRepLifetimeParams SharedParams;
    SharedParams.bIsPushBased = true;                    // <-- opt this property into push model

    DOREPLIFETIME_WITH_PARAMS_FAST(APlayerState, Score,             SharedParams);
    DOREPLIFETIME_WITH_PARAMS_FAST(APlayerState, bIsSpectator,      SharedParams);
    DOREPLIFETIME_WITH_PARAMS_FAST(APlayerState, PlayerNamePrivate, SharedParams);

    // You can reuse the params struct and tweak conditions per-property:
    SharedParams.Condition = COND_SkipOwner;
    DOREPLIFETIME_WITH_PARAMS_FAST(APlayerState, CompressedPing,    SharedParams);

    SharedParams.Condition = COND_InitialOnly;
    DOREPLIFETIME_WITH_PARAMS_FAST(APlayerState, PlayerId,          SharedParams);
    // ...
}
```

The key differences from classic:
- `FDoRepLifetimeParams` with `bIsPushBased = true`.
- `DOREPLIFETIME_WITH_PARAMS_FAST` (the `_FAST` variant) instead of plain `DOREPLIFETIME`.

The property declaration in the header is unchanged:
```cpp
UPROPERTY(ReplicatedUsing = OnRep_Score)
float Score;
```

---

## Step 3 — Mark the property dirty whenever you change it

**This is the crucial behavioral change.** With push model, the engine **no longer compares** the value to a shadow copy. It assumes the value is unchanged *unless you tell it otherwise*. If you forget to mark it dirty, **it will not replicate**.

```cpp
void APlayerState::SetScore(float NewScore)
{
    if (Score != NewScore)
    {
        Score = NewScore;
        // Tell the replication system this property changed:
        MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY_FROM_NAME(APlayerState, Score, this);
    }
}
```

### The available macros

| Macro | When to use |
|---|---|
| `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY_FROM_NAME(ClassName, PropertyName, Object)` | Most common. You know the class + property by name. |
| `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY(Object, Property)` | When you already have the `FProperty*`. |
| `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY_UNSAFE(Object, RepIndex)` | Low-level, used internally (e.g. in `RepLayout.cpp`); you supply the rep index directly. |
| `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY_FROM_NAME_STATIC_ARRAY_INDEX(...)` | For marking a specific index of a static array property. |

You can see all of these used across the engine — e.g. `SceneComponent.cpp` marks `bVisible`, `StaticMeshComponent.cpp` marks `StaticMesh`, etc.

---

## Putting it all together — minimal example

```cpp
// MyActor.h
UCLASS()
class AMyActor : public AActor
{
    GENERATED_BODY()
public:
    AMyActor() { bReplicates = true; }

    void SetHealth(float NewHealth);

    UPROPERTY(ReplicatedUsing = OnRep_Health)
    float Health = 100.f;

    UFUNCTION()
    void OnRep_Health();

    virtual void GetLifetimeReplicatedProps(TArray<FLifetimeProperty>&) const override;
};
```

```cpp
// MyActor.cpp
#include "Net/UnrealNetwork.h"
#include "Net/Core/PushModel/PushModel.h"   // for MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY

void AMyActor::GetLifetimeReplicatedProps(TArray<FLifetimeProperty>& OutLifetimeProps) const
{
    Super::GetLifetimeReplicatedProps(OutLifetimeProps);

    FDoRepLifetimeParams Params;
    Params.bIsPushBased = true;
    DOREPLIFETIME_WITH_PARAMS_FAST(AMyActor, Health, Params);
}

void AMyActor::SetHealth(float NewHealth)
{
    // Only the authority should change replicated state
    if (HasAuthority() && Health != NewHealth)
    {
        Health = NewHealth;
        MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY_FROM_NAME(AMyActor, Health, this);
    }
}
```

---

## What actually changes under the hood

Recall from our previous discussion: classic `FObjectReplicator::ReplicateProperties()` re-reads **every** replicated property and **compares** to a per-connection shadow buffer.

With push model:
- The comparison is **skipped**. Instead, an `FRepChangedPropertyTracker` / net push dirty-state holds a **dirty bitfield** per object.
- `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY` sets the bit. `ReplicateProperties()` only serializes properties whose dirty bit is set.
- After sending, the relevant state is cleared.

So the win is: **the expensive per-property comparison disappears**, replaced by O(1) "is this bit set?" checks. This matters most for objects with many properties and/or many connections.

> ⚠️ **Important gotcha**: this is per-object dirty tracking shared across connections in spirit, but the change history is still managed per connection for reliability/late-joiners. The dirty *mark* is what you control; the engine still handles delivering it to each connection that needs it.

---

## Common pitfalls

1. **Forgetting `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY`** → the property silently never replicates. The #1 push-model bug.
2. **Mixing**: a property declared push-based but changed in many places — you must mark dirty in *every* place it's mutated (including direct field writes, array adds, etc.).
3. **Mutating from non-authority** — only the authority's marks matter; client writes won't replicate anyway.
4. **Build vs. CVar mismatch** — if `net.IsPushModelEnabled 0` or `bWithPushModel=false`, marks are no-ops and it falls back to comparison (so it still *works*, just without the perf win — which can mask missing marks during testing!).

---

## How to verify it's actually on

- Console: `net.IsPushModelEnabled` (prints current value).
- There are net profiling/verification CVars (e.g. push-model validation modes) that compare push results against the old comparison path to catch missing dirty marks. If you want, I can search your engine source for the exact validation CVar name available in 5.8.

---

Would you like to go deeper into:
1. **The internals** — `FRepChangedPropertyTracker` / `FNetPushObjectHandle` and how the dirty bit flows into `ReplicateProperties()`?
2. The **validation/debug CVars** to catch missing dirty marks?
3. Or move on to how this concept becomes the *default* mindset in **Iris**?

Assistant:

Let me ground this in your actual UE 5.8 engine source.Let me explore the Iris source structure so I can ground this in your actual engine code.The Iris source is outside the project root (it's in the engine tree), so I can't tree-walk it directly, but I confirmed the key module paths. You're on UE 5.8 with the full Iris source under `Engine/Source/Runtime/Net/Iris/`. Let me teach you Iris at the **same architectural level** we did for classic replication, mapping each classic piece to its Iris counterpart.

# Iris Replication — the same-level walkthrough

Where classic replication is **per-(actor, connection)** and **object-oriented**, Iris is **centralized** and **data-oriented**. The whole mental model shifts from *"each actor's channel diffs itself per connection"* to *"one system holds all replicated state as data and computes what to send to whom in batched passes."*

---

## The big structural inversion

Classic:
```
UNetDriver → UNetConnection → UActorChannel(per actor) → FObjectReplicator(per object) → diff
```

Iris:
```
UReplicationSystem (1, central)
   ├─ NetRefHandleManager        (all replicated objects as data / "NetObjects")
   ├─ ReplicationStateDescriptors (per-class flattened state layout)
   ├─ ReplicationStateStorage     (the actual state buffers, SoA-ish)
   ├─ Filtering / Prioritization / Conditionals (batched passes)
   └─ ReplicationWriter/Reader (per connection — serialize the already-computed deltas)
```

The actor channel is **gone** as the unit of work. There's no per-actor `FObjectReplicator` doing per-connection comparisons.

---

## 1. `UReplicationSystem` — the central brain (replaces the per-connection loop)

This is the top-level object (in `Iris/ReplicationSystem/ReplicationSystem.h`). There can be more than one (the test plugin literally tests "MultiReplicationSystems"), but conceptually it's **the** owner of all replication.

It owns the subsystems and drives the per-frame pipeline:
1. **Poll/copy** dirty state from game objects into Iris state buffers.
2. **Quantize** the state (convert to the network representation once — not per connection!).
3. **Filtering** — which objects are relevant to which connections.
4. **Prioritization** — order objects by importance under the bandwidth budget.
5. **Serialization** — write the deltas per connection via the `ReplicationWriter`.

The crucial difference from classic: steps 1–2 happen **once per object**, not once per (object × connection).

---

## 2. `FNetRefHandleManager` / `FNetRefHandle` — objects as data (replaces NetGUID + UActorChannel identity)

In classic, an object's network identity is a `FNetGUID` and its per-connection presence is the `UActorChannel`. In Iris:

- Every replicated object gets a **`FNetRefHandle`** — a lightweight handle (an index, basically), not a pointer.
- The `FNetRefHandleManager` keeps a **dense, contiguous array** of "NetObjects." This is the data-oriented core: objects are rows in arrays, addressed by index, so the engine can iterate them cache-coherently and in parallel.

So "all replicated objects" is a flat table, not a graph of channels. This is what makes filtering/prioritization fast — they operate over arrays with bitsets.

---

## 3. `FReplicationStateDescriptor` — the flattened state layout (the Iris analog of `FRepLayout`)

In classic, `FRepLayout` is the cached per-class description of replicated properties. In Iris, the equivalent is the **`FReplicationStateDescriptor`** (built by the `ReplicationStateDescriptorBuilder`).

It describes, per class/struct:
- Each replicated member, its offset, and its **NetSerializer** (see below).
- Change-mask bit positions (the dirty bits).
- Conditionals (the `COND_` lifetime conditions, now expressed as condition bits).

This is generated once per type and shared. It's richer than `FRepLayout` because it's also used to *quantize* state into a network-internal form.

---

## 4. `FNetSerializer` — the per-type serialization unit (replaces ad-hoc property net serialization)

This is one of the most important new concepts. In classic replication, property serialization is scattered (property-type switch logic, `NetSerialize` overrides, etc.). Iris formalizes it:

> A **`FNetSerializer`** is a declarative, reusable object that knows how to **Quantize**, **Dequantize**, **Serialize**, **Deserialize**, and **compare/IsEqual** one type.

So `float`, `FVector`, `FRotator`, enums, structs all have NetSerializers. Your `UPROPERTY(Replicated)` members get matched to NetSerializers when the descriptor is built. Custom types declare one via `UE_NET_IMPLEMENT_SERIALIZER` / the `Net.Serialization` macros.

The **Quantize** step is key: state is converted to its compact network form **once**, stored, and then the same quantized bytes are written to every connection that needs it. Compare to classic, where serialization happens per channel.

---

## 5. The change model — Push is *mandatory*, not optional

Remember Push Model from our last topic? In classic it was an **opt-in optimization**. In Iris, **dirty tracking is the foundation** — there is no full per-connection comparison fallback as the primary path.

- Each NetObject has a **change mask** (a bitset, one region per replicated member/fragment).
- When game code marks state dirty (the same `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY` macros bridge into Iris), the corresponding bits flip.
- The poll step copies only dirty members; serialization only writes set bits to each connection.

So everything you learned about push model is the *normal operating mode* in Iris. The `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY_FROM_NAME(...)` calls you write work in **both** systems — that's the deliberate compatibility bridge.

> There's also a "polling" path (Iris can poll certain objects' state and detect changes itself for legacy compatibility), but the architecture is built around the change mask, not per-connection shadow-buffer comparison.

---

## 6. Filtering & Prioritization — batched, centralized passes (replaces per-connection relevancy in `ServerReplicateActors`)

In classic, relevancy + `NetPriority` are computed inside the per-connection actor loop (or in the Replication Graph). In Iris these are **first-class, batched subsystems**:

- **Filtering** (`NetObjectFilter` / `ReplicationFiltering`): operates over the NetObject array with **bitsets per connection** ("which of these N objects does connection C get?"). Includes things like distance/grid filters, owner filters, group filters. Because it's bitset math over arrays, it scales much better than per-actor `IsNetRelevantFor` virtual calls.
- **Prioritization** (`NetObjectPrioritizer`): assigns priorities so the bandwidth budget is spent on the most important objects, again as a batched pass.
- **Conditionals** (`ReplicationConditionals`): the `COND_OwnerOnly`/`COND_SkipOwner`/etc. become condition bits evaluated centrally.

---

## 7. `FReplicationWriter` / `FReplicationReader` — the per-connection serialization (the only strongly per-connection piece)

This is where Iris *does* touch each connection — but only to **assemble the packet from already-quantized state**:

- The `FReplicationWriter` (one per connection) takes the filtered+prioritized list of dirty NetObjects and writes their quantized, change-masked state into the connection's data stream, respecting the bandwidth budget. It also tracks per-connection acknowledgment / resend state (`FNetObjectInfo`-style records).
- On the receiving side, `FReplicationReader` applies incoming state into the client's NetObject state and then **dequantizes** into the actual `UObject` members, firing RepNotifies.

Contrast with classic: there, the *comparison itself* was per connection (expensive). Here, the comparison/quantization already happened once centrally; the per-connection step is "select bits and pack," which is much cheaper.

---

## 8. RPCs and bridging — `UObjectReplicationBridge`

Iris doesn't replace the whole engine overnight; it bridges to actors via the **`UObjectReplicationBridge`** (and `ReplicationSystemUtil`, which is the file I found in `Engine/Private/Net/Iris/ReplicationSystem/`). The bridge:
- Registers `AActor`/`UActorComponent` instances as NetObjects (assigning `FNetRefHandle`s).
- Translates between the engine's actor lifecycle (BeginPlay/Destroy, dormancy, sub-objects) and Iris's NetObject create/destroy.
- Handles RPCs by routing them through Iris's reliable/unreliable channels while keeping the familiar `Server`/`Client`/`Multicast` UFUNCTION semantics.

This is why your gameplay code mostly doesn't change.

---

## Side-by-side mapping (the cheat sheet)

| Classic concept | Iris concept | Note |
|---|---|---|
| `UNetDriver` (replication loop) | `UReplicationSystem` | Centralized pipeline instead of per-conn loop |
| `FNetGUID` + identity | `FNetRefHandle` + `FNetRefHandleManager` | Objects are array rows, not channels |
| `UActorChannel` (per actor/conn) | *(gone)* — NetObject + per-conn writer | No per-actor channel object |
| `FObjectReplicator` (per object/conn diff) | Change mask + `FReplicationStateDescriptor` | Diff is central + push-based |
| `FRepLayout` | `FReplicationStateDescriptor` | Flattened, plus quantize info |
| ad-hoc property serialize | `FNetSerializer` (Quantize/Serialize) | Declarative per-type, quantize once |
| Push Model (opt-in) | Change mask (mandatory) | Same `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY` macros |
| relevancy / `NetPriority` in loop / RepGraph | `NetObjectFilter` / `NetObjectPrioritizer` | Batched bitset passes |
| `FOutBunch` per channel | `FReplicationWriter` per connection | Packs pre-quantized state |
| — | `UObjectReplicationBridge` | Glues actors ↔ Iris |

---

## Where to read the actual code (in your tree)

Since you have the source, the high-value files are:
- `Engine/Source/Runtime/Net/Iris/Public/Iris/ReplicationSystem/ReplicationSystem.h` — the top-level brain.
- `Engine/Source/Runtime/Net/Iris/Public/Iris/ReplicationSystem/ReplicationSystemTypes.h` — handles/types.
- `Engine/Source/Runtime/Engine/Public/Net/Iris/ReplicationSystem/ReplicationSystemUtil.h` + the `.cpp` — the actor bridge layer.
- The `ReplicationSystemTestPlugin` — excellent **readable examples** of registering objects, filtering, and the writer/reader in isolation (great for learning).

---

The single most important takeaway: **Iris makes the work happen *per object once* (poll → quantize → mark dirty), and only the cheap "pick bits + pack" step is per connection** — that's the whole reason it scales.

Good next steps to go one level deeper:
1. **`FNetSerializer` in detail** — Quantize/Dequantize and how to write one for a custom type.
2. **The change mask + descriptor** — how `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY` flows into Iris bits.
3. **Filtering/prioritization** internals (the bitset passes).
4. **How to actually enable Iris** in your `AZ` project's config and verify it's running.

Which would you like next? Given your goal of understanding *both* deeply, I'd suggest **#2 (change mask + descriptor)** since it's the direct continuation of the push-model topic we just covered.

Either way, here's the **complete summary content** ready to use. Just save this as `.md`:

```markdown
# UE5 Networking: Classic Actor Replication vs. Iris

A study summary comparing the two replication systems in Unreal Engine 5.

---

## 1. Classic Actor Replication — Core Model

Built around: **`UNetDriver` + `UActorChannel` + `FObjectReplicator`**

### Per-tick flow
```
UNetDriver::TickFlush()
  └─ ServerReplicateActors()                 // WHAT to send, to WHOM
       └─ for each UNetConnection
            └─ for each relevant actor
                 └─ UActorChannel::ReplicateActor()
                      └─ FObjectReplicator::ReplicateProperties()  // diff + serialize
                           └─ writes into FOutBunch
```

### Key actors
- **`UNetDriver`** — one per world (GameNetDriver). Owns connections, drives the
  per-tick replication loop, owns the GuidCache (object ↔ NetGUID) and optional
  ReplicationGraph (relevancy/prioritization).
- **`UNetConnection`** — one per client. Owns channels, packs bunches into packets,
  handles reliability/ordering/ACKs. State diffing is **per connection**.
- **`UActorChannel`** — one per (Actor, Connection). Owns FObjectReplicators, handles
  actor spawn/destroy over the wire.
- **`FObjectReplicator`** — the property diff engine for a single UObject. Holds a
  per-connection shadow buffer of last-sent values; compares current vs. shadow each pass.

### Supporting types
- **`FRepLayout`** — cached, flattened per-class description of replicated properties.
- **`FRepState` / `FRepChangelistState`** — per-object/per-connection change tracking.
- **`FRepChangedPropertyTracker`** — tracks conditions + (push model) dirty flags.

### Cost characteristic
- Default = **poll/compare**: re-reads & compares EVERY replicated property,
  for EVERY connection → O(connections × properties).

---

## 2. Push Model (classic optimization, bridge to Iris)

Two parts must BOTH be in place:

### Build/config
```csharp
// *.Target.cs
bWithPushModel = true;   // on by default for editor/dev builds
```
```ini
; DefaultEngine.ini
[SystemSettings]
net.IsPushModelEnabled=1
```
Console: `net.IsPushModelEnabled 1`

### Declare properties as push-based
```cpp
void AMyActor::GetLifetimeReplicatedProps(TArray<FLifetimeProperty>& Out) const
{
    Super::GetLifetimeReplicatedProps(Out);

    FDoRepLifetimeParams Params;
    Params.bIsPushBased = true;
    DOREPLIFETIME_WITH_PARAMS_FAST(AMyActor, Health, Params);
}
```

### Mark dirty on every change (CRUCIAL)
```cpp
void AMyActor::SetHealth(float NewHealth)
{
    if (HasAuthority() && Health != NewHealth)
    {
        Health = NewHealth;
        MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY_FROM_NAME(AMyActor, Health, this);
    }
}
```

### Macros
- `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY_FROM_NAME(ClassName, PropertyName, Object)` — most common
- `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY(Object, Property)` — when you have the FProperty*
- `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY_UNSAFE(Object, RepIndex)` — low-level
- `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY_FROM_NAME_STATIC_ARRAY_INDEX(...)` — static array element

### Under the hood
- Comparison is **skipped**; a dirty bitfield is set by the macro.
- Only dirty properties are serialized. O(1) "is bit set?" instead of comparison.

### Pitfalls
1. Forgetting `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY` → property silently never replicates (#1 bug).
2. Must mark dirty in EVERY place the value is mutated.
3. Only authority's marks matter.
4. Build/CVar off → marks are no-ops, falls back to comparison (can mask missing marks!).

---

## 3. Iris Replication — Core Model

Centralized + data-oriented (vs. classic's per-(actor,connection) object-oriented model).

### Structural inversion
```
UReplicationSystem (1, central)
  ├─ NetRefHandleManager          (all replicated objects as data rows)
  ├─ ReplicationStateDescriptors  (per-class flattened state layout)
  ├─ ReplicationStateStorage      (actual quantized state buffers)
  ├─ Filtering / Prioritization / Conditionals (batched bitset passes)
  └─ ReplicationWriter/Reader     (per connection — pack pre-quantized state)
```
The per-actor channel is **gone** as the unit of work.

### Per-frame pipeline
1. **Poll/copy** dirty state from game objects → Iris buffers.
2. **Quantize** state to network form — ONCE per object (not per connection!).
3. **Filtering** — which objects relevant to which connections (bitsets).
4. **Prioritization** — order by importance under bandwidth budget.
5. **Serialization** — per-connection ReplicationWriter packs the deltas.

Steps 1–2 happen **once per object**, not per (object × connection) → the key win.

### Key concepts
- **`UReplicationSystem`** — central brain, owns subsystems + pipeline.
- **`FNetRefHandle` / `FNetRefHandleManager`** — objects as lightweight handles
  (indices) in dense arrays → cache-coherent, parallel-friendly.
- **`FReplicationStateDescriptor`** — Iris analog of FRepLayout; adds quantize info
  + change-mask bit positions + conditionals.
- **`FNetSerializer`** — declarative per-type unit: Quantize / Dequantize / Serialize /
  Deserialize / IsEqual. Quantize once, reuse bytes for all connections.
- **Change mask** — push dirtiness is MANDATORY (foundation, not opt-in). Same
  `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY` macros bridge in.
- **Filtering / Prioritization / Conditionals** — batched, centralized bitset passes
  (vs. per-connection relevancy in classic).
- **`FReplicationWriter` / `FReplicationReader`** — only strongly per-connection piece;
  assembles packets from already-quantized state.
- **`UObjectReplicationBridge`** — glues Actors/Components ↔ Iris NetObjects, handles
  lifecycle + RPCs so gameplay code mostly doesn't change.

---

## 4. Side-by-Side Mapping

| Classic concept                         | Iris concept                          |
|-----------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|
| `UNetDriver` (replication loop)         | `UReplicationSystem`                   |
| `FNetGUID` + identity                   | `FNetRefHandle` + `FNetRefHandleManager` |
| `UActorChannel` (per actor/conn)        | (gone) NetObject + per-conn writer     |
| `FObjectReplicator` (per object/conn)   | Change mask + `FReplicationStateDescriptor` |
| `FRepLayout`                            | `FReplicationStateDescriptor`          |
| ad-hoc property serialize               | `FNetSerializer` (Quantize/Serialize)  |
| Push Model (opt-in)                     | Change mask (mandatory)                |
| relevancy / NetPriority / RepGraph      | `NetObjectFilter` / `NetObjectPrioritizer` |
| `FOutBunch` per channel                 | `FReplicationWriter` per connection    |
| —                                       | `UObjectReplicationBridge`             |

---

## 5. Key Takeaways

- **Classic**: work scales O(connections × properties) because the diff/serialize
  happens per (object × connection). Push Model optimizes the *comparison* but keeps
  the per-connection channel structure.
- **Iris**: poll → quantize → mark-dirty happen **per object once**; only the cheap
  "pick bits + pack" step is per connection. That's why Iris scales to large player
  counts / many objects.
- **Compatibility**: `UPROPERTY(Replicated)`, `GetLifetimeReplicatedProps`, and
  `MARK_PROPERTY_DIRTY` work in BOTH systems — the deliberate bridge.

---

## 6. Where to Read the Source (UE 5.8)

- `Engine/Source/Runtime/Net/Iris/Public/Iris/ReplicationSystem/ReplicationSystem.h`
- `Engine/Source/Runtime/Net/Iris/Public/Iris/ReplicationSystem/ReplicationSystemTypes.h`
- `Engine/Source/Runtime/Engine/Public/Net/Iris/ReplicationSystem/ReplicationSystemUtil.h`
- `ReplicationSystemTestPlugin` — readable isolated examples.
```

    FGameplayTag Item_Property_Stackable;           // Can stack multiple units in one slot.

    FGameplayTag Item_Property_Droppable;           // Can be dropped from inventory.
    FGameplayTag Item_Property_QuestItem;           // Cannot be dropped, sold, or destroyed.
    FGameplayTag Item_Property_Tradeable;           // Can be sold or exchanged with NPCs.
    FGameplayTag Item_Property_Craftable;           // Can be used in crafting recipes.
    FGameplayTag Item_Property_Breakable;           // Has durability and can break on use.
    FGameplayTag Item_Property_Cursed;              // Has negative or supernatural side effects.
    FGameplayTag Item_Property_Hidden;              // Hidden or inactive until revealed.
    FGameplayTag Item_Property_LimitedUse;          // Has limited number of uses or charges.
    FGameplayTag Item_Property_Fragile;             // Easily breaks or loses effectiveness.

    // Defines quality, drop rate, and thematic importance.
    FGameplayTag Item_Rarity_Common;                // Everyday or easily found item.

# UE5 Networking: Classic Actor Replication vs. Iris

A study summary capturing a full discussion comparing the two replication systems
in Unreal Engine 5 (context: UE 5.8, built from source).

> Tip: Convert this file to PDF with VS Code's "Markdown PDF" extension,
> `pandoc Networking_Iris_vs_Actor.md -o Networking_Iris_vs_Actor.pdf`,
> or any Markdown previewer's "Print → Save as PDF".

---

## Table of Contents

1. [Overview: Two Replication Systems](#1-overview-two-replication-systems)
2. [Classic Actor Replication — Core Model](#2-classic-actor-replication--core-model)
3. [Push Model (Classic Optimization & Bridge to Iris)](#3-push-model-classic-optimization--bridge-to-iris)
4. [Iris Replication — Core Model](#4-iris-replication--core-model)
5. [Side-by-Side Mapping](#5-side-by-side-mapping)
6. [Key Takeaways](#6-key-takeaways)
7. [Where to Read the Source (UE 5.8)](#7-where-to-read-the-source-ue-58)
